Not messages like words, exactly. Just… signs. The trucks slowing, people looking over — it showed me they knew the neighborhood had been picked for it. For my last big civic piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong need to keep working on the display, even when you didn't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did feel like I had to keep going. Even after dark, even when I was worn out.\n\nThey say I used our savings on the wood and lights and all that, but at the time it felt like if I stopped, the house… the whole block maybe… would lose its chance.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do things in a specific way while working on the display?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really a specific way, no. I just kept at it. Hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Did you have thoughts about the display that repeated over and over in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I thought about it a lot, sure. But I don’t know if it was the same thought over and over like you mean. It was just… on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Did you believe that the attention from neighbors or the slowing trucks was specifically about you or your project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I know folks think I’m confused about it, but it felt pretty clear to me.\n\nWhen the trucks slowed down, or the neighbors stood there looking… that wasn’t just nothing. It meant they knew. Like the neighborhood had been chosen for what I was making. My last big civic piece, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for your project was not true, even for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not really. People say I’m mixed up, I know that. But to me it was clear. The trucks slowing down, neighbors watching… those things meant they knew. It was picked for that piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't work on the display?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I did. Not like harm exactly, but like… if I stopped, the house and the block would miss its chance.\n\nThey say I spent our savings on wood and lights and kept going after dark. But it felt like I had to keep at it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you couldn't stop thinking about the display even when you wanted to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I thought about it a lot, sure. But whether I couldn’t stop… I’m not sure how to answer that. It was just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong urge to perform certain actions repeatedly while working on the display?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I kept working on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a repeated urge like that. It was just… what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your ideas about the neighborhood being chosen for your project might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: Not really. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but… I’m pretty sure the neighborhood was picked for it. Little things showed it—trucks slowing down, neighbors looking over. They knew.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

They knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever consider that the neighborhood being chosen for your project was just your own idea and not based on any real evidence?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know folks think I’m mixed up, but I’m sure the neighborhood was picked for this. My last big civic art piece, you know.\n\nLittle things showed it—trucks slowing down, neighbors looking over. They knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that someone or something was sending you messages or signals through the trucks slowing down or neighbors looking over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I did. Not messages like words, exactly. Just… signs. The trucks slowing, people looking over — it showed me they knew the neighborhood had been picked for it. For my last big civic piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong need to keep working on the display, even when you didn't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did feel like I had to keep going. Even after dark, even when I was worn out.\n\nThey say I used our savings on the wood and lights and all that, but at the time it felt like if I stopped, the house… the whole block maybe… would lose its chance.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do things in a specific way while working on the display?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really a specific way, no. I just kept at it. Hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Did you have thoughts about the display that repeated over and over in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I thought about it a lot, sure. But I don’t know if it was the same thought over and over like you mean. It was just… on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Did you believe that the attention from neighbors or the slowing trucks was specifically about you or your project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. Like the neighborhood had been chosen for what I was making. It was picked for that piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't work on the display?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I did. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my family says I needed to come in. They say I spent too much of our savings on wood and lights and things for a display, and I kept working on it after dark.\n\nI don’t know. It felt important. Like if I stopped, the house… maybe the whole block… would miss its chance.\n\nDoctor: Did you really believe that the house or the block would miss its chance if you stopped working on the display?\n\nPatient: I… yes. I know folks think I’m mixed up, but I’m sure the neighborhood was picked for this. My last big civic art piece, you know.\n\nLittle things showed it—trucks slowing down, neighbors looking over. They knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that someone or something was sending you messages or signals through the trucks slowing down or neighbors looking over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I suppose I did. Not messages like words, exactly. Just… signs. The trucks slowing, people looking over — it showed me they knew the neighborhood had been picked for it. For my last big civic piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong need to keep working on the display, even when you didn't want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did feel like I had to keep going. Even after dark, even when I was worn out.\n\nThey say I used our savings on the wood and lights and all that, but at the time it felt like if I stopped, the house… the whole block maybe… would lose its chance.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you had to do things in a specific way while working on the display?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really a specific way, no. I just kept at it. Hard to explain.\n\nDoctor: Did you have thoughts about the display that repeated over and over in your mind?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean… I thought about it a lot, sure. But I don’t know if it was the same thought over and over like you mean. It was just… on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Did you believe that the attention from neighbors or the slowing trucks was specifically about you or your project?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I know folks think I’m confused about it, but it felt pretty clear to me.\n\nWhen the trucks slowed down, or the neighbors stood there looking… that wasn’t just nothing. It meant they knew. Like the neighborhood had been chosen for what I was making. My last big civic piece, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your belief about the neighborhood being chosen for your project was not true, even for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… not really. People say I’m mixed up, I know that. But to me it was clear. The trucks slowing down, neighbors watching… those things meant they knew. It was picked for that piece.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like something bad would happen if you didn't work on the display?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I did. Not like harm exactly, but like… if I stopped, the house and the block would miss its chance.\n\nThey say I spent our savings on wood and lights and kept going after dark. But it felt like I had to keep at it.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel like you couldn't stop thinking about the display even when you wanted to think about something else?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I thought about it a lot, sure. But whether I couldn’t stop… I’m not sure how to answer that. It was just kind of there.\n\nDoctor: Did you feel a strong urge to perform certain actions repeatedly while working on the display?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I kept working on it, but I don’t know if I’d call it a repeated urge like that. It was just… what I was doing.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your ideas about the neighborhood being chosen for your project might not be based in reality?\n\nPatient: Not really. I know people think I’m mixed up about it, but… I’m pretty sure the neighborhood was picked for it. Little things showed it—trucks slowing down, neighbors looking over. They knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever consider that the neighborhood being chosen for your project was just your own idea and not based on any real evidence?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I mean, maybe other people don’t see it, but to me there were signs. Trucks slowing down, people looking over… things like that. It wasn’t just something I made up.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that other people didn't see the signs you mentioned because they weren't paying attention, rather than because the signs weren't really there?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I figured people just weren’t looking close enough. I mean, I know they think I’m mixed up, but those signs were there. The trucks slowing down, neighbors watching… that told me they knew about the neighborhood being picked.\n\nDoctor: Did you believe that the attention from the neighbors or the slowing trucks was specifically about your project or you in a way that went beyond a normal or rational explanation?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I did. I know how it sounds, but it wasn’t just normal traffic or neighbors being nosy. It felt like they knew about the project… like the neighborhood had been picked for it, and those little things proved it.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your belief about the neighborhood being chosen was false, even if just for a moment?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I really did. I know people say I’m mixed up, but I was sure about it. The trucks slowing down, the neighbors looking over… that meant something. It proved they knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your beliefs about the neighborhood and the project were not based on real events or facts?\n\nPatient: No… not really. I know people think I’m mixed up, but I’m sure about it. The neighborhood was picked for my last big civic art piece, and those little things—trucks slowing down, neighbors looking over—those were real. They knew.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever consider seeking evidence to prove that the neighborhood was actually chosen for your project?\n\nPatient: I mean… I didn’t really need to go looking. The evidence was kind of right there to me. The trucks slowing down, neighbors watching… that showed they knew the neighborhood was picked for my project. People say I’m mixed up, but I was sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever think that your beliefs about the project and the neighborhood were wrong or unrealistic?\n\nPatient: No… I didn’t think it was wrong.
